href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastor2015[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjoanofarchistory0000cast_n6r7page97_97],_[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjoanofarchistory0000cast_n6r7page168_168]Gies1981[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjoanofarclegendr0000giespage54_54],_[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjoanofarclegendr0000giespage154_154]PernoudClin1986[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjoanofarcherstor00pernpage30_30–31],_[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjoanofarcherstor00pernpage105_105]-385">&#91;378&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Joan has been described as a model of an autonomous woman who challenged traditions of masculinity and femininity<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDworkin1987&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsintercourse0000dworpage123_123–125&#93;Sullivan1996&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsfreshverdictsonj0000unsepage103_103&#93;_386-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDworkin1987[httpsarchiveorgdetailsintercourse0000dworpage123_123–125]Sullivan1996[httpsarchiveorgdetailsfreshverdictsonj0000unsepage103_103]-386">&#91;379&#93;</a></sup> to be heard as an individual<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarstow1985&#91;httpswwwjstororgstable40003571seq1_24–29&#93;_387-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarstow1985[httpswwwjstororgstable40003571seq1_24–29]-387">&#91;380&#93;</a></sup> in a patriarchal culture<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarstow1985&#91;httpswwwjstororgstable40003571seq1_24–29&#93;_387-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarstow1985[httpswwwjstororgstable40003571seq1_24–29]-387">&#91;380&#93;</a></sup>—setting her own course by heeding the voices of her visions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarstow1986&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsjoanofarcheretic0000barspage127_127–129&#93;_388-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarstow1986[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjoanofarcheretic0000barspage127_127–129]-388">&#91;381&#93;</a></sup> She fulfilled the traditionally male role of a military leader,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDworkin1987&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsintercourse0000dworpage104_104–105&#93;Fraioli1981&#91;httpswwwjstororgstable2847364seq7_817&#93;Sproles1996&#91;httpswwwjstororgstable23539706seq5_162&#93;Taylor2012&#91;httpswwwjstororgstable23240136seq2_217&#93;Warner1981&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsjoanofarcimageof0000warnpage216_216&#93;_389-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDworkin1987[httpsarchiveorgdetailsintercourse0000dworpage104_104–105]Fraioli1981[httpswwwjstororgstable2847364seq7_817]Sproles1996[httpswwwjstororgstable23539706seq5_162]Taylor2012[httpswwwjstororgstable23240136seq2_217]Warner1981[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjoanofarcimageof0000warnpage216_216]-389">&#91;382&#93;</a></sup> while maintaining her status as a valiant woman.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDworkin1987&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsintercourse0000dworpage104_104&#93;_390-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDworkin1987[httpsarchiveorgdetailsintercourse0000dworpage104_104]-390">&#91;383&#93;</a></sup> Merging qualities associated with both genders,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarstow1985&#91;httpswwwjstororgstable40003571seq4_29&#93;_391-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarstow1985[httpswwwjstororgstable40003571seq4_29]-391">&#91;384&#93;</a></sup> Joan has <a href="/wiki/Cultural_depictions_of_Joan_of_Arc" title="Cultural depictions of Joan of Arc">inspired numerous artistic and cultural works</a> for many centuries. In the nineteenth century, hundreds of work of art about her—including biographies, plays, and musical scores—were created in France, and her story became popular as an artistic subject in Europe and North America.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDunn2021&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidSG4HEAAAQBAJpgPA38_38&#93;_392-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunn2021[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidSG4HEAAAQBAJpgPA38_38]-392">&#91;385&#93;</a></sup> By the 1960s, she was the topic of thousands of books.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELightbody1961&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsjudgementsofjoan0000lighpage16_16–17&#93;_393-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELightbody1961[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjudgementsofjoan0000lighpage16_16–17]-393">&#91;386&#93;</a></sup> Her legacy has become global, and inspires novels, plays, poems, operas, films, paintings, children's books, advertising, computer games, comics and popular culture across the world.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECohen2014&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDcfbAgAAQBAJpgPA110_110&#93;_394-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2014[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDcfbAgAAQBAJpgPA110_110]-394">&#91;387&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-39"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-39">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Fauquembergue's doodle on the margin of a Parliament's register is the only known contemporary representation of Joan. <li id="cite_note-269"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-269">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In the foreground of this allegorical work, Guillaume Bouillé, who opened the inquest, is handing Joan, who died twenty years previously but is symbolically present, the text of her rehabilitation. The figures in the background are <a href="/wiki/Jean_Br%C3%A9hal" title="Jean Bréhal">Jean Bréhal</a> (standing), the inquisitor; <a href="/wiki/Jean_Juv%C3%A9nal_des_Ursins" title="Jean Juvénal des Ursins">Jean Juvénal des Ursins</a>, archbishop of Reims (enthroned in the center); and one of the other commissioners (enthroned), either <a href="/wiki/Guillaume_Chartier_(bishop)" title="Guillaume Chartier (bishop)">Guillaume Chartier (bishop)</a>, bishop of Paris or <a href="/wiki/Richard_Olivier_de_Longueil" title="Richard Olivier de Longueil">Richard Olivier de Longueil</a>, bishop of Coutances.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELGPC2022_268-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELGPC2022-268">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup></span>

<li id="cite_note-312"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-312">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> For example, <a href="#CITEREFMackowiak2007">Mackowiak 2007</a>, pp.&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://archive.org/details/postmortemsolvin0000mack/page/138">138–139</a> points out problems with assuming Joan had schizophrenia, ergot poisoning or temporal lobe issues; <a href="#CITEREFHughes2005">Hughes 2005</a>, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.epilepsybehavior.com/article/S1525-5050(04)00354-3/fulltext">abstract</a> disputes the conjecture that she had epilepsy; <a href="#CITEREFNoresYakovleff1995">Nores &amp; Yakovleff 1995</a>, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.karger.com/Article/Abstract/119218">abstract</a> argue against her visions being caused by tuberculosis; one of Joan's advocates at the canonization trial pointed out that her case did not meet the clinical descriptions of hysteria;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly1996&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailsfreshverdictsonj0000unsepage222_222&#93;_311-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly1996[httpsarchiveorgdetailsfreshverdictsonj0000unsepage222_222]-311">&#91;305&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="#CITEREFRatnasuriya1986">Ratnasuriya 1986</a>, pp.&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/pdf/10.1177/014107688607900413">234–235</a> critiques diagnosing Joan as a creative psychopath.</span>

Mais c'est précisément cette exactitude, et cette coïncidence, trop belle pour être vraie, qui éveillent—ou plutôt auraient dû éveiller—les soupçons<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... [This miniature from the 15th century, very neat (the banner corresponds exactly to the description that Joan of Arc herself will give during her trial)<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... <li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTENapier2017&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidH2AuDwAAQBApgPT62_67&#93;-293"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTENapier2017[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidH2AuDwAAQBApgPT62_67]_293-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFNapier2017">Napier 2017</a>, p.&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=H2AuDwAAQBA&amp;pg=PT62">67</a>; see <a href="#CITEREFBréhal1456">Bréhal 1456</a>, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://archive.org/details/jean-brehal-grand-inquisiteur-de-france/page/104">pt I, ch. VIII (p. 104-105) </a>: <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Unde, quatinus ille episcopus et alii in hoc ei faventes se a malicia manifesta contra ecclesiam romanam , aut etiam ab heresi , se debite excusare possent, non video.</i></span> [How that bishop [Cauchon] and others who favored him in this respect [that is, in continuing the trial] can excuse themselves from malice toward the Roman Church, or even from heresy, I cannot see.]</span>
</li>
